Emotion-trajectory path map

A map of which emotional transitions are actually available in a 500-voice speech corpus, measured in the space the emotion scorer actually produced rather than the space the prompt labels claim — plus 100 assembled listening examples so you can hear what the map buys.
Built 2026-08-17 on JUPITER. Nothing on this page has been validated by ear.

If you read one box, read this one A trajectory is a planned emotional journey: a short performance that starts in one feeling and travels to another through intermediate steps, so the emotion moves instead of sitting still. Each example below is 3–6 short clips by the same synthetic voice, glued together, chosen so that consecutive clips are close together in a 40-dimensional "emotion space".

Three things this page establishes, all of which were surprises:
  1. Putting the 40 emotion dimensions on a common scale changes the units by 2.09×. The step limits of 1.5 and 1.6 that were asked for turn out, in the normalised space, to be roughly half the size of the 1.3 used previously — not a relaxation but a severe tightening. Taken literally they leave 98.7 % and 97.4 % of all emotion pairs unreachable.
  2. A single clip's measured emotion is nearly useless on its own. The typical clip sits 6.12 units away from the centre of its own condition, while two different conditions are only 5.00 apart on average. The noise is bigger than the signal it would have to resolve.
  3. At the aggregate level the space is nevertheless sane. Average enough clips and 71 of the 80 emotion×intensity states have their own emotion as the top measured dimension, and the space clusters into recognisable neighbourhoods (hostility, distress, warmth, elation).
  1. What you are listening to
  2. Normalising the 40 dimensions
  3. The scale trap: 1.5 and 1.6 are not what they look like
  4. Coverage: all 1,560 ordered pairs
  5. Label versus measurement
  6. Single-take noise, and how the nodes were built
  7. How each collage was assembled
  8. What is not established
  9. The 100 examples

1 · What you are listening to

The corpus is 500 synthetic voices. Each voice was asked to perform every one of 40 emotions at four settings — the settings vary intensity (intense / moderate) and containment (open / contained) — in English and German, roughly 48 takes per combination. That is 20,125,736 recorded takes in total. Every take was then scored by an emotion model (Empathic Insight) which outputs 40 numbers, one intensity per emotion.

So every take is a point in a 40-dimensional space. A state in this page is a pair like intense Anger or moderate Thankfulness — 40 emotions × 2 intensities = 80 states. A step is a move from one state to another, and its size is the ordinary straight-line distance between those two states in the 40-dimensional space. A trajectory is a chain of states where every step is below some limit, so no single move is a jump.

The question this page answers is: given a limit on how big one step may be, which emotional journeys are possible at all? Anger to Thankfulness in one move is a jump. Is there a route — Anger, then Sourness, then Interest, then Contentment, then Thankfulness — where every move is small? And is the route from Thankfulness back to Anger the same route? (It is not, in general; that is why all 1,560 ordered pairs are computed, not 780 unordered ones.)

One decision that shapes everything below An earlier design positioned each arc using the intended prompt label — "this take was asked for Anger, therefore it is Anger". This page does not. Every position here comes from the measured emotion scores of the actual audio. If takes generated as intense Anger do not measure as intense anger, that shows up as a distorted map instead of being hidden behind the label. The intended label is kept as metadata on every clip so you can see both, and section 5 reports exactly where the two disagree.

2 · Normalising the 40 dimensions

The 40 emotion outputs are not on a comparable scale. Before any distance can mean anything, each dimension has to be expressed in units of its own spread — otherwise the whole geometry is decided by whichever heads happen to swing widest, and an emotion the model rarely fires at all becomes invisible.

The constants were derived by streaming every meta parquet of all 500 voices (20,125,736 rows; 41,561 rows flagged empty — failed or zero-length generations — were excluded, leaving 20,084,175). They are frozen in emotion_norm_constants.json and every number on this page uses them.

z_d(take) = ( emonet_d(take) - MEAN_d ) / SD_d       # MEAN, SD over all 20,084,175 takes

The spread across dimensions is 3.19× — from 0.218 (Intoxication Altered States of Consciousness) to 0.697 (Hope Enthusiasm Optimism). The means differ far more: Interest sits at 1.87 while Intoxication sits at 0.0101. Interest is essentially always on; that is a known property of this scorer and normalisation is what stops it dominating.

Sorted by sd. "Share of state distance" is the fraction of the total squared distance between the 80 states that this dimension accounts for; 2.50 % would be an equal share. Kurtosis and max z are computed on a 96,036-take random sample of the quality pool.

One dimension is near-degenerate, and it was checked rather than assumed harmless Intoxication Altered States of Consciousness is the problem case: mean 0.0101, sd 0.2181, and 94.6 % of all takes score below 0.05. It is not a dimension with small variance so much as a dimension that is almost always exactly off and occasionally spikes — kurtosis 17.3, and after z-scoring its largest single-take value is 17.1 standard deviations. That is exactly the situation where naive z-scoring turns a rare artefact into a dominant coordinate.

At the take level it does: a single clip with a spurious intoxication reading is thrown 17 units off. At the state level — which is what the map is built on, and which averages thousands of takes — it does not. It contributes only 0.163 % of the squared distance between states, the smallest share of all 40 dimensions. Deleting it entirely moves the median state-to-state distance from 5.000 to 4.991, a Spearman correlation of 0.99991 with the full-space distances, and changes 5 of 446 edges at the operating threshold. It was therefore kept, with the caveat recorded that per-take distances involving it should not be trusted. Three further dimensions are heavy-tailed in the same way but less extremely — Infatuation (kurtosis 93), Shame (74) and Embarrassment (58).

What normalisation actually changed

Quantityraw spacenormalised spaceReading
Median distance between two of the 80 states2.3895.000the whole space inflates by 2.093× — this is the single most consequential change
Rank order of those 3,160 distancesSpearman 0.9580the shape of the space barely moves
States whose nearest neighbour changes2 of 80local structure is essentially preserved
Take-level 40-way emotion recovery24.31 %25.40 %a clip's nearest state centroid has the right emotion slightly more often (chance 2.5 %)
Take-level 80-way state recovery15.42 %16.14 %chance 1.25 %

So normalisation is not a cosmetic rescaling and not a reshaping. It leaves the geometry almost exactly where it was (Spearman 0.958) while multiplying every distance by 2.09. That single fact is what breaks the thresholds, and it is the subject of the next section. It also buys a small genuine improvement in how often a clip lands nearest to its own label (24.3 % → 25.4 %), which is the direction you would want but is nowhere near enough to make per-clip labels usable.

The largest individual movers are pairs involving the sparse, heavy-tailed dimensions — Sexual Lust, Shame and Embarrassment. Their raw values are small numbers, so raw-space distance treats them as near-identical; normalised, their differences count properly and those pairs move apart by up to about 1.0 raw-equivalent units.

3 · The scale trap: 1.5 and 1.6 are not what they look like

The brief asked for the step limit to be raised from the previously used 1.3 to 1.5 and 1.6, expecting fewer hops and rougher transitions. That expectation is correct if the units stay the same. They do not. Normalisation multiplied every distance by 2.093, so:

Limit as writtenin raw unitsin normalised unitsWhat it actually is
prior study 1.3 (raw)1.302.72the old operating point
1.5, read literally in the normalised space0.7171.50a 2.1× tightening, not a relaxation
1.6, read literally in the normalised space0.7641.60likewise
1.5, rescaled to mean the same thing1.503.14the intended "conservative" point
1.6, rescaled to mean the same thing1.603.35the intended "relaxed" point

Both readings are reported below. The literal reading is reported because it is what was asked for and because its emptiness is itself the finding. The rescaled reading — normalised 3.14 and 3.35 — is what the collages were built at, because the literal thresholds cannot produce 100 varied trajectories: at literal 1.5 only 20 of 1,560 ordered emotion pairs are reachable at all, and they are concentrated in two or three small huddles of the space.

4 · Coverage: all 1,560 ordered pairs

Method. Build a graph on the 80 states; connect two states if the distance between their measured centres is at or below the limit. Then for each of the 40 × 39 = 1,560 ordered emotion pairs, find the shortest chain of states from any intensity of the source emotion to any intensity of the target, by breadth-first search. "1 hop" means a direct step exists. "3" means the journey needs two intermediate states. "Unreachable" means no chain of any length exists.

4.1 The two thresholds as literally written

Limit (normalised)edges among 80 statesmean degreeisolated states1 hop234+unreachablecoverage
1.50 (= 0.72 raw)421.05201820015401.3 %
1.60 (= 0.76 raw)571.4363082015202.6 %
At the literal thresholds the map is essentially empty At 1.50, 42 edges exist among 80 states, 20 states have no neighbour at all, and 1540 of 1,560 ordered emotion pairs (98.7 %) cannot be reached by any route. At 1.60 it improves to 57 edges and 1520 unreachable pairs (97.4 %).

Listing the unreachable pairs would be listing almost everything, so here is the complement — the complete set of emotion pairs that are reachable:

Reachable at normalised 1.50 — 20 of 1,560 ordered pairs:
Amusement → Teasing, Concentration → Contemplation, Concentration → Interest, Confusion → Doubt, Contemplation → Concentration, Contemplation → Interest, Distress → Helplessness, Doubt → Confusion, Elation → Hope Enthusiasm Optimism, Elation → Pride, Helplessness → Distress, Hope Enthusiasm Optimism → Elation, Hope Enthusiasm Optimism → Pride, Impatience & Irritability → Sourness, Interest → Concentration, Interest → Contemplation, Pride → Elation, Pride → Hope Enthusiasm Optimism, Sourness → Impatience & Irritability, Teasing → Amusement

States with no neighbour at all at 1.50 (20 of 80): intense Affection, moderate Affection, intense Amusement, intense Contentment, moderate Contentment, moderate Distress, intense Fatigue Exhaustion, moderate Fatigue Exhaustion, intense Hope Enthusiasm Optimism, intense Pleasure Ecstasy, moderate Pleasure Ecstasy, intense Pride, intense Sexual Lust, moderate Sexual Lust, intense Shame, moderate Shame, intense Thankfulness Gratitude, moderate Thankfulness Gratitude, intense Triumph, moderate Triumph

Reachable at normalised 1.60 — 40 of 1,560 ordered pairs:
Affection → Infatuation, Amusement → Teasing, Anger → Contempt, Concentration → Contemplation, Concentration → Interest, Confusion → Doubt, Contemplation → Concentration, Contemplation → Interest, Contempt → Anger, Disappointment → Distress, Disappointment → Fear, Disgust → Impatience & Irritability, Disgust → Sourness, Distress → Disappointment, Distress → Fear, Distress → Helplessness, Doubt → Confusion, Elation → Hope Enthusiasm Optimism, Elation → Pleasure Ecstasy, Elation → Pride, Fear → Disappointment, Fear → Distress, Helplessness → Distress, Hope Enthusiasm Optimism → Elation, Hope Enthusiasm Optimism → Pleasure Ecstasy, Hope Enthusiasm Optimism → Pride, Impatience & Irritability → Disgust, Impatience & Irritability → Sourness, Infatuation → Affection, Interest → Concentration, Interest → Contemplation, Pleasure Ecstasy → Elation, Pleasure Ecstasy → Hope Enthusiasm Optimism, Pleasure Ecstasy → Pride, Pride → Elation, Pride → Hope Enthusiasm Optimism, Pride → Pleasure Ecstasy, Sourness → Disgust, Sourness → Impatience & Irritability, Teasing → Amusement

States with no neighbour at all at 1.60 (6 of 80): intense Affection, intense Fatigue Exhaustion, moderate Fatigue Exhaustion, intense Pleasure Ecstasy, intense Sexual Lust, moderate Sexual Lust

The pattern is legible. What survives at these limits are the pairs that are near-synonyms in the measured space: Confusion ↔ Doubt, Concentration ↔ Contemplation ↔ Interest, Amusement ↔ Teasing, Distress ↔ Helplessness, Elation ↔ Pride ↔ Hope. Everything else in the corpus is further apart than 1.6 normalised units, which after rescaling is only 0.76 in the old raw units — well under half the old step limit.

The isolated states are informative, and they both confirm and refine the prior study's finding. That study, working in raw units on 40 pooled emotion centroids from 8 voices, named Triumph and Thankfulness as the two isolates. Splitting emotions by intensity sharpens the picture. At 1.50, Triumph and Thankfulness are isolated outright. At 1.60 they are technically no longer isolated — but their only neighbour is themselves at the other intensity (intense Triumph ↔ moderate Triumph at 1.54; intense ↔ moderate Thankfulness at 1.52). They remain completely cut off from every other emotion, which is why neither appears anywhere in the list of reachable pairs above. Thankfulness is the extreme case in the whole corpus: its nearest different emotion is intense Contentment, at 2.37 from moderate Thankfulness and 3.63 from intense Thankfulness. Triumph is less remote than the prior study suggested — intense Pride sits 1.89 from moderate Triumph.

The states that are isolated in the strict sense at 1.60 — intense Affection, intense Fatigue Exhaustion, moderate Fatigue Exhaustion, intense Pleasure Ecstasy, intense Sexual Lust, moderate Sexual Lust — are a different and previously unnamed set. Fatigue/Exhaustion and Sexual Lust are so remote that even their own two intensity settings are further apart than 1.60. Both are heavy-tailed, sparsely-fired dimensions (section 2), so it is worth holding open the possibility that this is a property of the scorer rather than of the acting.

4.3 Conservative versus relaxed — the trade, on evidence

Quantityconservative — 3.14 (= 1.5 raw)relaxed — 3.35 (= 1.6 raw)change
Edges among the 80 states446520+74 (+17 %)
Mean neighbours per state11.1513.00+1.85
Pairs reachable in 1 hop358400+42
Pairs needing 2 hops510582+72
Pairs needing 3 hops488444-44
Pairs needing 4+ hops204134-70 (−34 %)
Mean path length (hops)2.372.21−0.16
Longest shortest path65−1
Unreachable pairs00—

This is the trade the brief asked to see, and it is real but modest. Relaxing from 3.14 to 3.35 (1.5 → 1.6 in old units) buys 17 % more edges, shortens the mean route by 0.16 hops, and cuts the hardest cases — pairs needing four or more hops — by 34 %. In exchange, every step is permitted to be 6.7 % larger. Both cover all 1,560 pairs; the choice is entirely about how rough an individual seam is allowed to be, which is exactly the thing this page cannot settle by measurement and can only put in front of your ears.

For reference, the prior study's operating point rescales to 2.72, where 298 pairs (19 %) are unreachable and mean degree is only 6.42. Full coverage of the 1,560 pairs first appears at 3.00. That is a stronger argument for raising the threshold than the one the brief made: the old limit does not merely make paths long, it makes 298 of them impossible.

5 · Label versus measurement

Every take carries two claims: the label it was generated under ("intense Anger") and the measurement the scorer returned. This section reports where they disagree, at the level of the 80 states — that is, after averaging thousands of takes, which is the only level at which the measurement is stable enough to ask.

The test is simple: in a state's measured centre, is that state's own emotion dimension the largest one? For 71 of the 80 states it is. The median rank of a state's own dimension is 1. That is a real endorsement of the corpus at the aggregate level — the emotion adapters mostly fired, and mostly fired at the right thing.

The 9 exceptions are worth naming individually, because they are the states where a trajectory labelled one thing is measurably somewhere else:

Statez of its own dimensionrank of own dimensionWhat it actually measures asz of that
moderate Intoxication Altered States of Consciousness+0.5713Fatigue Exhaustion+1.23
moderate Concentration+0.256Hope Enthusiasm Optimism+0.42
moderate Emotional Numbness+1.574Sadness+1.75
moderate Interest+0.323Affection+0.33
intense Intoxication Altered States of Consciousness+0.913Confusion+1.03
intense Anger+1.942Impatience & Irritability+1.98
moderate Anger+1.542Impatience & Irritability+1.67
moderate Distress+1.372Helplessness+1.39
moderate Sourness+0.902Impatience & Irritability+0.92
Two failures and one near-miss Intoxication is a genuine failure: asked for it moderately, the corpus produces something the scorer reads as Fatigue / Exhaustion (own dimension ranked 13th of 40); asked for it intensely, it reads as Confusion. Given that Intoxication is also the near-degenerate dimension of section 2, the most likely explanation is that the head barely works, not that the acting is wrong. Moderate Concentration is the second failure — own dimension ranked 6th, measuring instead as Hope/Enthusiasm.

The rest are near-misses that are arguably correct. Anger, at both intensities, measures marginally higher on Impatience & Irritability than on Anger itself (+1.98 vs +1.94 intense). Moderate Distress peaks on Helplessness; moderate Sourness on Impatience; moderate Emotional Numbness on Sadness. These are the scorer's nearest semantic neighbours, losing by a few hundredths. That is a labelling ambiguity, not a broken performance.

At the other end, the states that land most emphatically on their own label are intense Shame (+5.39), intense Thankfulness Gratitude (+4.87), intense Fatigue Exhaustion (+4.58), intense Sexual Lust (+4.12). Note that Thankfulness — flagged by the prior study as possibly a mis-calibrated dimension because it sits far from everything — is in fact the most confidently measured state in the corpus. It is far from the others because it is distinctive, not because it is broken.

6 · Single-take noise, and how the nodes were built

The instruction was to build the graph on measured take vectors rather than label centroids. Taken completely literally — nodes are individual takes, an edge exists when two takes are within the threshold — the graph is empty. Measured on 6,000 randomly sampled quality takes (17.8 M pairs):

Distance between two takes, normalised spacep05medianp95within 1.5within 3.14
Two takes of the same state5.418.8913.510.002 %< 0.1 %
Two takes of different states6.4110.2715.270.000 %< 0.1 %
For comparison: two state centres2.345.007.951.3 %28 %
The measurement noise is larger than the thing it would have to measure A typical take sits 6.12 units from the centre of its own condition. Two different conditions are typically 5.00 units apart. In other words, an individual clip is further from where its own label says it should be than two randomly chosen labels are from each other. Two takes asked for the same thing are median 8.89 apart; two takes asked for different things are 10.27 apart. The distributions overlap almost completely.

A per-take gate of the kind "only use takes within radius R of their own condition's centre" is therefore not available: R = 4.0 keeps 4.7 % of the pool, R = 3.0 keeps 0.1 %, and R = 2.0 keeps none at all. There is no radius that is both meaningful and survivable.

What was done instead. Each node is the measured centre of a quality-gated pool: for every (emotion × intensity) state, the mean normalised emotion vector of the top-5 takes per condition group across all 500 voices, with a 4-second duration floor applied before ranking. That is 800,000 takes contributing to 80 nodes, about 10,000 per node. Averaging that many takes is what makes the position stable; sections 4 and 5 are computed on those nodes.

What that costs, stated plainly. The trajectory is a real object about the corpus; the individual clip you hear is a noisy sample from a very wide distribution around the point it is meant to occupy. To narrow that as far as the material allows, each clip in each collage was chosen as the closest of its voice's quality pool to the node it fills — not the highest-scoring take, the nearest-measuring one, out of the 10 takes available for that voice, language and state. That moves the typical clip from 6.12 units from its node down to 4.49.

It does not fix the problem. 4.49 is still substantially larger than the 3.14 step limit the whole map is built on. Concretely: the clip-level realisation of a trajectory is dominated by noise, and any given seam may not move in the direction the map says it does. What selection does buy is measurable — the chosen clips agree with their intended emotion (measured argmax equals intended label) 35.5 % of the time, against 25.4 % for a random quality take, and 91 % of them have their intended emotion above the corpus mean.

7 · How each collage was assembled

Every number below is a decision that could have gone another way, so each is stated with its reason.

StepWhat was doneWhy
Candidate pool4.0 s duration floor applied before ranking, then top-5 per condition group by the pipeline composite score.The prior study measured that ranking on genuineness + burst blend alone picks a take under 3 s 12.6 % of the time — one "best" Anger take was a 0.1 s clip saying "Yeah". The shortest clip used anywhere on this page is 4.16 s.
Clip choiceOf the 10 quality takes for that voice, language and state, the one whose measured normalised vector is closest to the node.Section 6. This is the only noise control the material supports.
Silence trimEnergy-based. 20 ms frames, RMS in dB. Threshold = the clip's own 95th-percentile frame level minus 35 dB, clamped to a −60 dBFS floor. Cut to the first and last frame above threshold, then 30 ms of the original signal restored at each end as a pad. A clip with no frame above threshold is rejected outright.A per-clip reference rather than a global constant, because clip loudness varies by about 4.6 dB p05→p95. The 30 ms pad exists so a plosive or breath onset is not clipped off the front of a word, which is far more audible than a 30 ms gap. Measured result: mean 57 ms removed at the head, 56 ms at the tail, p95 297 / 243 ms, worst case 1.13 s — 1.24 % of total duration.

Audited on 40 clips against an independent fixed −45 dBFS threshold: the relative rule lands at a median of −50.2 dBFS (range −53 to −47), the quietest frame in a typical clip is −89 dBFS (true digital silence) and the 95th-percentile speech frame is −15.2 dBFS, so the threshold sits far above the floor and far below speech. The two methods agree on the head to the frame (0.040 s median each) and differ by more than 150 ms on 1 clip of 40. The removed fraction is well below the 4.12 % the prior study measured on unselected candidates, because the 4-second floor and the closest-to-node choice both select against truncated, silence-padded takes.

What this does not fix: only the head and tail are trimmed. Checking the finished audio of 30 collages, 29 contain at least one internal quiet stretch longer than 400 ms — median longest 580 ms, worst 1.52 s — sitting inside a single source take, several of them true digital silence. Those are pauses the TTS produced mid-utterance, and they were deliberately left alone. A listener may well hear one of them as a seam.
LoudnessEach clip measured with EBU R128 integrated loudness (pyloudnorm) and normalised to −23 LUFS, but preserving up to ±3 dB of its original deviation from the collage's own mean. The finished file is peak-limited to −1.0 dBFS.Loudness is part of emotion — a shouted line should stay louder than a whispered one — so full normalisation would flatten exactly what the page is about. ±3 dB is a factor of two in power: audible as dynamics, not as a mistake. Achieved: mean absolute level step across a seam 1.15 dB, max 5.09 dB; 6 of 334 seams exceed 4 dB, which is the ±3 dB window doing its job rather than a defect.
Join15 ms equal-power fade-out, a fixed 150 ms silence, 15 ms fade-in. No crossfade.A 50–200 ms crossfade would blend two different phonations and leave an audible doubling at every seam — a signature artefact. 15 ms is shorter than any phoneme and kills the waveform-discontinuity click. The 150 ms gap is a fixed beat rather than a random one so that collages remain comparable to each other; a production build would randomise it.
Encoding128 kbps MP3, mono, 48 kHz, embedded in this page as data: URIs.The source is 160 kbps dual-mono at 48 kHz, so mono loses nothing. The page needs no server and works offline.

Sampling

100 collages: 50 conservative (limit 3.14 = 1.5 raw) and 50 relaxed (3.35 = 1.6 raw). Lengths 3 clips × 32, 4 clips × 20, 5 clips × 30, 6 clips × 18. Languages 42 English / 58 German — never mixed inside a collage. 100 distinct voices, so almost no voice is heard twice.

Trajectories were drawn to cover the space rather than one corner of it: the 80 states were clustered into 8 regions, and the fill sample walks the region × region matrix, with no source→target emotion pair used twice within a threshold. 24 of the 100 are deliberate mirror pairs — six emotion pairs built in both directions at both thresholds, so you can hear whether direction matters: Anger ↔ Thankfulness, Sadness ↔ Elation, Contempt ↔ Affection, Fear ↔ Pride, Shame ↔ Triumph, Amusement ↔ Distress. They are tagged mirror below.

8 · What is not established

No part of this has been validated by ear. That is what the audio below is for.
  • Per-clip emotion recovery is about 25 % against a chance rate of 2.5 %. Better than chance, nowhere near reliable. A clip's own 40-number vector is too noisy to place that clip on the map (section 6), so the audio may simply not carry the arc the chain claims.
  • The map is a claim about the corpus, not about perception. "Within 3.14 normalised units" means two states' average measured scores are close. Whether a listener hears that as a smooth transition is entirely unmeasured. The whole point of choosing 3.14 versus 3.35 is a perceptual question and this page contains no perceptual evidence.
  • The nodes are pooled across all 500 voices; the audio is from one voice at a time. A given voice's Anger may sit somewhere quite different from the corpus-wide Anger. This was not checked per-voice.
  • Speaker continuity across seams was not gated. The prior study established that the speaker embedder has a 24.3 % equal-error rate on this material and that gating on it removes exactly the loud, high-arousal takes an emotional arc needs. No seam here was rejected for sounding like a different person; some may.
  • Intoxication and moderate Concentration are known-bad nodes (section 5). Any trajectory routed through them is routed through a measurement that does not mean what its name says.
  • The 150 ms seam gap is uniform and artificial. Real emotional transitions have breaths and beats of varying length. Every collage here has the same rhythm at every seam, which may itself read as an edit.
  • Some clips contain longer internal pauses than the seams do. Trimming is head-and-tail only; 29 of 30 audited collages carry an internal quiet stretch over 400 ms (worst 1.52 s) inside a single source take. If you hear a break where the chain says there is none, that is why — and it means "can you hear the seams?" is not a clean question on this material.
  • Language was not controlled against emotion. German and English takes were pooled into the same nodes. If the emotion heads behave differently by language, that difference is inside every node.

What would settle it: a listening test in which people hear a collage and are asked whether the emotion moves, in which direction, and whether any seam sounds like an edit — with the conservative and relaxed versions of the same trajectory presented blind. The mirror pairs and the paired thresholds below were built so that test can be run directly off this page.

9 · The 100 examples

Each card gives the trajectory as a chain of states with the size of each step, the threshold it was built at, the voice, and the concatenated per-clip captions. There is deliberately no whole-collage caption: the captions are the source clips' own procedural descriptions, in order, one per component — nothing was written to describe the collage as a whole.

Total audio 66 minutes, mean 39 s per collage, 63 MB of MP3 served from traj_audio/ alongside this page.
